    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… um, I guess I’m here because I don’t feel like myself anymore. Especially with work. I used to be good at it—I got promoted, people trusted me—but now I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, like something bad is just… built into it now.\n\nAnd it’s weird, because I know offices are just offices, and half the time people are talking about printer issues or someone’s terrible lunch in the break room, but in my head it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like I’m different now. Damaged, maybe. Like the competent version of me everyone thinks they’re talking to isn’t really there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling that something bad is built into your work?\n\nPatient: It’s like… I know logically work is just work, right? Spreadsheets, tickets, meetings that could’ve been emails. But since everything changed, I can’t feel that way anymore. It’s like the whole place has this warning label on it in my head.\n\nAnd then I start thinking maybe it’s not even the job, maybe it’s me. Like I’m not the person they promoted. I used to feel capable, like if something went wrong I could handle it. Now I feel… cracked or something. Damaged. Like everyone is still treating me like I’m competent, but I’m kind of pretending to be that person.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things at work even when there's no apparent reason to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do worry, but it’s not always about one specific thing that makes sense. It’s more like this background feeling that work just isn’t safe anymore.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there doing something totally normal—checking a system report or answering an email—and my brain treats it like I’m walking into something dangerous. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just work. I used to be good at this stuff. I got promoted, I knew what I was doing.\n\nNow I keep thinking maybe the problem is me. Like something in me got damaged, and the competent person everyone thinks they’re dealing with isn’t really there anymore. And then I’ll start worrying about that instead of the actual task, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour thinking in circles.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it that way. Work is the place I notice it most, probably because it’s where I’m supposed to be functioning and, you know, sounding normal on calls and answering people like I have everything together.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, I think about stupid regular things, like whether I bought groceries or if I need to do laundry, and then somehow I’m thinking about how I forgot to defrost chicken last week and had cereal for dinner, which is not really what you asked. Sorry.\n\nI guess I don’t know if I’d call it the same kind of worrying outside work. It’s just harder for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things outside of work in the same way that you worry about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if it’s the same way. Like… outside work I can get stuck thinking about things, but it feels more scattered? Like bills, or whether I replied to a text, or if my apartment is a mess because I keep meaning to clean the bathroom sink and then I notice the lightbulb in the hallway is out, and then I’m on some website comparing lightbulbs for twenty minutes.\n\nBut that’s not exactly the same as the work feeling. Work has this… weight to it. Outside work it’s more like noise, I guess. I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this same level of worry if you were to change jobs or leave your current work environment?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, like if I just changed jobs it would go away, because that would be cleaner, you know? Like when your laptop is acting weird and you think, okay, maybe if I just restart it everything will be fine. Sorry, that’s a dumb comparison.\n\nBut I don’t know. I’m here because I can’t shake this feeling that work itself isn’t safe anymore. Not just this office or this team. It’s like something about being in that role, having responsibilities, people expecting me to be competent… it makes me feel like I’m going to get exposed somehow.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m the thing that’s different now. Like the person who got promoted and knew what she was doing isn’t really there anymore, or she’s damaged or something. So if I left, maybe I’d feel relieved for a little while, but I’m scared I’d take that with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're alone, without any work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not exactly in a “responsibility” way, though.\n\nEven when I’m not doing anything important, I catch myself sort of scanning. Like on the train I’m checking where the doors are, who’s standing too close, whether someone is behind me. And in meetings—sorry, that is work—but even at lunch with my team, when I’m supposed to just be eating a sandwich and laughing about whatever show people are watching, I’m half listening for hallway noises or watching the entrance.\n\nWhen I’m alone it’s quieter, so sometimes I notice it more, if that makes sense. Like I’m not relaxed-relaxed. I’m still kind of… ready for something. Even if nothing is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more or less anxious when you're in a completely familiar and comfortable environment, like your own home?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… at home it’s less intense than, like, the train or the office. But I wouldn’t say it totally shuts off.\n\nLike, if I’m home and it’s quiet, I can still catch myself noticing every little sound in the hall, or thinking about where I left my keys, which is not the same thing, sorry. I guess the clearest place I notice it is out in public or at work—on the train, in meetings, even just eating lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on hallway noises instead of actually being there.\n\nAt home it’s more familiar, so I’m not doing it as obviously, but I don’t know if I ever feel completely off-duty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're engaging in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, it depends what “enjoy” means, because I still go through the motions, like I’ll be at lunch with my team and everyone’s joking, or I’m on the train listening to a podcast I usually like, and part of me is trying to be normal and enjoy it.\n\nBut then I realize I’m not really in it. I’m automatically looking for exits, noticing who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on noises in the hallway or behind me. Even in meetings, which I know aren’t exactly fun, I’ll catch myself doing the same thing instead of actually focusing.\n\nSo yes, I guess even when it’s supposed to be a normal or pleasant thing, I’m still kind of… monitoring everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or work-related responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can be thinking about nothing work-related at all—like just riding the train, or sitting with my team at lunch, and someone’s talking about their weekend or some random show—and I’ll still be… not relaxed.\n\nI automatically notice where the exits are, who’s too close to me, if someone’s behind me. In meetings too, even when I’m not focused on the actual work part, I’m listening for hallway sounds or watching the door. It’s like part of my brain stays busy with that, even when I’m supposed to just be present.\n\nSorry, I know that kind of circles back to work places, but it’s not really about work responsibilities. It’s more like wherever I am, I’m checking the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if you didn't have to work at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would go away just because I wasn’t working. Maybe some parts would be less, like not having meetings, or deadlines, or the office hallways—actually the hallway at work has this echo that makes every footstep sound closer than it is, which is probably not what you asked.\n\nBut the checking thing happens outside of work too. On the train, at lunch with my team, in meetings, I’m automatically looking for exits, noticing who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on noises around me instead of actually being there. So if I didn’t work at all, maybe there’d be fewer situations where it shows up, but I don’t think the anxiety would decrease significantly. It feels more like I carry it into places, not just that work causes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly less if you were in a completely different work environment or role?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, depending on the environment. Like if it were quieter, or I didn’t have those long hallways—sorry, that sounds oddly specific, but hallways really do make me… aware.\n\nBut I don’t think a different role would make it significantly less. Because I do it everywhere. On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of actually being fully present.\n\nSo if the job were different, maybe the setting would bother me less in certain moments, but the scanning thing would probably still come with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a situation where you are completely in control and there are no external expectations or pressures?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Even if there wasn’t anything expected of me, I don’t know that I’d feel totally calm.\n\nLike, I could be sitting somewhere with nothing to do—no deadlines, no one needing anything—and I’d still notice where the exits are. Or who’s too close to me. I do that on the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team when people are just talking about normal stuff. And then I’m half-listening for footsteps or hallway noise instead of actually being there.\n\nSo… control helps a little maybe, but it doesn’t turn that part of my brain off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to take a break from work for an extended period of time, like a vacation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. A vacation sounds like it should help, and maybe it would in the sense that I wouldn’t have emails or meetings—well, unless I checked my email anyway, which I’m trying not to do, but that’s a whole other thing.\n\nBut even when I’m not working, I still do that scanning thing. Like on the train, or sitting with my team at lunch, or in meetings—actually meetings aren’t vacation, sorry. I mean, even in normal places, I’m automatically noticing the exits, watching if someone is standing too close, keeping one ear on hallway sounds or footsteps instead of really settling in.\n\nSo I think time off might make the work pressure less, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel completely relaxed. That alert feeling would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be the same if you were in a completely different environment, unrelated to work?\n\nPatient: I think it would be… not exactly the same, maybe, but pretty close. Like the place changes, but my brain still does the same routine.\n\nEven on the train, or when I’m in a meeting, or just at lunch with my team—sorry, that’s still work-adjacent, but even when it’s casual—I’m automatically checking where the exits are, noticing who’s standing too near me, listening for hallway sounds instead of just being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s kind of annoying because part of me knows nothing is happening, but I’m still doing the scan anyway. So a different environment might have different triggers, I guess, but I don’t think it would just go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you were to change your job or work environment to one that is very quiet and has minimal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? If it was quieter and there weren’t as many responsibilities, I’d probably have less work stuff to react to. Like fewer people needing things from me.\n\nBut I don’t think it would be significantly reduced, exactly, because I still do the same thing in different places. On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of really being present.\n\nAnd quiet can be weird too, actually—like sometimes when it’s too quiet, I notice every little sound more. Sorry, that’s kind of a tangent. I just mean… changing jobs might take off some pressure, but I don’t think it would shut off that alertness.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety is more related to your own thoughts and feelings or to the things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… both, but maybe it starts in me? Like, nothing has to be happening exactly, and I’ll still be doing it.\n\nOn the train, in meetings, even just having lunch with my team, I automatically clock where the exits are, and I notice who’s standing too close, and I’m half-listening for hallway noises instead of fully paying attention. Which is frustrating, because someone could just be talking about, I don’t know, a software rollout or what they brought for lunch, and I’m sitting there acting normal but my brain is doing security checks.\n\nSo it’s not only what’s around me. The surroundings kind of give me things to latch onto, but the anxious part feels like it’s already running in the background.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I feel completely calm that often. I mean, there are probably moments where I look calm, like I’m sitting at lunch with my team and nodding along, but part of me is still… checking things.

Like on the train, in meetings, even at lunch, I automatically clock the exits, who’s standing too close, and I’m half-listening to noises in the hallway instead of really being present. It’s kind of like when my laptop has a background process running and everything seems fine until you notice it’s draining the battery—sorry, work analogy. But that’s what it feels like.

So, no, not completely relaxed most of the time. There are quieter moments, but I’m usually still a little on alert.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… um, I guess I’m here because I don’t feel like myself anymore. Especially with work. I used to be good at it—I got promoted, people trusted me—but now I can’t shake this feeling that work isn’t safe anymore, like something bad is just… built into it now.\n\nAnd it’s weird, because I know offices are just offices, and half the time people are talking about printer issues or someone’s terrible lunch in the break room, but in my head it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like I’m different now. Damaged, maybe. Like the competent version of me everyone thinks they’re talking to isn’t really there anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this feeling that something bad is built into your work?\n\nPatient: It’s like… I know logically work is just work, right? Spreadsheets, tickets, meetings that could’ve been emails. But since everything changed, I can’t feel that way anymore. It’s like the whole place has this warning label on it in my head.\n\nAnd then I start thinking maybe it’s not even the job, maybe it’s me. Like I’m not the person they promoted. I used to feel capable, like if something went wrong I could handle it. Now I feel… cracked or something. Damaged. Like everyone is still treating me like I’m competent, but I’m kind of pretending to be that person.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things at work even when there's no apparent reason to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do worry, but it’s not always about one specific thing that makes sense. It’s more like this background feeling that work just isn’t safe anymore.\n\nLike I’ll be sitting there doing something totally normal—checking a system report or answering an email—and my brain treats it like I’m walking into something dangerous. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because it’s just work. I used to be good at this stuff. I got promoted, I knew what I was doing.\n\nNow I keep thinking maybe the problem is me. Like something in me got damaged, and the competent person everyone thinks they’re dealing with isn’t really there anymore. And then I’ll start worrying about that instead of the actual task, and suddenly I’ve lost half an hour thinking in circles.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really tracked it that way. Work is the place I notice it most, probably because it’s where I’m supposed to be functioning and, you know, sounding normal on calls and answering people like I have everything together.\n\nOutside of work… I mean, I think about stupid regular things, like whether I bought groceries or if I need to do laundry, and then somehow I’m thinking about how I forgot to defrost chicken last week and had cereal for dinner, which is not really what you asked. Sorry.\n\nI guess I don’t know if I’d call it the same kind of worrying outside work. It’s just harder for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about things outside of work in the same way that you worry about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if it’s the same way. Like… outside work I can get stuck thinking about things, but it feels more scattered? Like bills, or whether I replied to a text, or if my apartment is a mess because I keep meaning to clean the bathroom sink and then I notice the lightbulb in the hallway is out, and then I’m on some website comparing lightbulbs for twenty minutes.\n\nBut that’s not exactly the same as the work feeling. Work has this… weight to it. Outside work it’s more like noise, I guess. I haven’t really paid enough attention to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel this same level of worry if you were to change jobs or leave your current work environment?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, like if I just changed jobs it would go away, because that would be cleaner, you know? Like when your laptop is acting weird and you think, okay, maybe if I just restart it everything will be fine. Sorry, that’s a dumb comparison.\n\nBut I don’t know. I’m here because I can’t shake this feeling that work itself isn’t safe anymore. Not just this office or this team. It’s like something about being in that role, having responsibilities, people expecting me to be competent… it makes me feel like I’m going to get exposed somehow.\n\nAnd I keep thinking maybe I’m the thing that’s different now. Like the person who got promoted and knew what she was doing isn’t really there anymore, or she’s damaged or something. So if I left, maybe I’d feel relieved for a little while, but I’m scared I’d take that with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're alone, without any work or responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not exactly in a “responsibility” way, though.\n\nEven when I’m not doing anything important, I catch myself sort of scanning. Like on the train I’m checking where the doors are, who’s standing too close, whether someone is behind me. And in meetings—sorry, that is work—but even at lunch with my team, when I’m supposed to just be eating a sandwich and laughing about whatever show people are watching, I’m half listening for hallway noises or watching the entrance.\n\nWhen I’m alone it’s quieter, so sometimes I notice it more, if that makes sense. Like I’m not relaxed-relaxed. I’m still kind of… ready for something. Even if nothing is happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel more or less anxious when you're in a completely familiar and comfortable environment, like your own home?\n\nPatient: Less, I think… at home it’s less intense than, like, the train or the office. But I wouldn’t say it totally shuts off.\n\nLike, if I’m home and it’s quiet, I can still catch myself noticing every little sound in the hall, or thinking about where I left my keys, which is not the same thing, sorry. I guess the clearest place I notice it is out in public or at work—on the train, in meetings, even just eating lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on hallway noises instead of actually being there.\n\nAt home it’s more familiar, so I’m not doing it as obviously, but I don’t know if I ever feel completely off-duty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're engaging in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, it depends what “enjoy” means, because I still go through the motions, like I’ll be at lunch with my team and everyone’s joking, or I’m on the train listening to a podcast I usually like, and part of me is trying to be normal and enjoy it.\n\nBut then I realize I’m not really in it. I’m automatically looking for exits, noticing who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on noises in the hallway or behind me. Even in meetings, which I know aren’t exactly fun, I’ll catch myself doing the same thing instead of actually focusing.\n\nSo yes, I guess even when it’s supposed to be a normal or pleasant thing, I’m still kind of… monitoring everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're not thinking about work or work-related responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can be thinking about nothing work-related at all—like just riding the train, or sitting with my team at lunch, and someone’s talking about their weekend or some random show—and I’ll still be… not relaxed.\n\nI automatically notice where the exits are, who’s too close to me, if someone’s behind me. In meetings too, even when I’m not focused on the actual work part, I’m listening for hallway sounds or watching the door. It’s like part of my brain stays busy with that, even when I’m supposed to just be present.\n\nSorry, I know that kind of circles back to work places, but it’s not really about work responsibilities. It’s more like wherever I am, I’m checking the room.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if you didn't have to work at all?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would go away just because I wasn’t working. Maybe some parts would be less, like not having meetings, or deadlines, or the office hallways—actually the hallway at work has this echo that makes every footstep sound closer than it is, which is probably not what you asked.\n\nBut the checking thing happens outside of work too. On the train, at lunch with my team, in meetings, I’m automatically looking for exits, noticing who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on noises around me instead of actually being there. So if I didn’t work at all, maybe there’d be fewer situations where it shows up, but I don’t think the anxiety would decrease significantly. It feels more like I carry it into places, not just that work causes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly less if you were in a completely different work environment or role?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, depending on the environment. Like if it were quieter, or I didn’t have those long hallways—sorry, that sounds oddly specific, but hallways really do make me… aware.\n\nBut I don’t think a different role would make it significantly less. Because I do it everywhere. On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of actually being fully present.\n\nSo if the job were different, maybe the setting would bother me less in certain moments, but the scanning thing would probably still come with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a situation where you are completely in control and there are no external expectations or pressures?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Even if there wasn’t anything expected of me, I don’t know that I’d feel totally calm.\n\nLike, I could be sitting somewhere with nothing to do—no deadlines, no one needing anything—and I’d still notice where the exits are. Or who’s too close to me. I do that on the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team when people are just talking about normal stuff. And then I’m half-listening for footsteps or hallway noise instead of actually being there.\n\nSo… control helps a little maybe, but it doesn’t turn that part of my brain off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to take a break from work for an extended period of time, like a vacation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. A vacation sounds like it should help, and maybe it would in the sense that I wouldn’t have emails or meetings—well, unless I checked my email anyway, which I’m trying not to do, but that’s a whole other thing.\n\nBut even when I’m not working, I still do that scanning thing. Like on the train, or sitting with my team at lunch, or in meetings—actually meetings aren’t vacation, sorry. I mean, even in normal places, I’m automatically noticing the exits, watching if someone is standing too close, keeping one ear on hallway sounds or footsteps instead of really settling in.\n\nSo I think time off might make the work pressure less, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel completely relaxed. That alert feeling would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be the same if you were in a completely different environment, unrelated to work?\n\nPatient: I think it would be… not exactly the same, maybe, but pretty close. Like the place changes, but my brain still does the same routine.\n\nEven on the train, or when I’m in a meeting, or just at lunch with my team—sorry, that’s still work-adjacent, but even when it’s casual—I’m automatically checking where the exits are, noticing who’s standing too near me, listening for hallway sounds instead of just being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s kind of annoying because part of me knows nothing is happening, but I’m still doing the scan anyway. So a different environment might have different triggers, I guess, but I don’t think it would just go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you were to change your job or work environment to one that is very quiet and has minimal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? If it was quieter and there weren’t as many responsibilities, I’d probably have less work stuff to react to. Like fewer people needing things from me.\n\nBut I don’t think it would be significantly reduced, exactly, because I still do the same thing in different places. On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of really being present.\n\nAnd quiet can be weird too, actually—like sometimes when it’s too quiet, I notice every little sound more. Sorry, that’s kind of a tangent. I just mean… changing jobs might take off some pressure, but I don’t think it would shut off that alertness.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety is more related to your own thoughts and feelings or to the things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… both, but maybe it starts in me? Like, nothing has to be happening exactly, and I’ll still be doing it.\n\nOn the train, in meetings, even just having lunch with my team, I automatically clock where the exits are, and I notice who’s standing too close, and I’m half-listening for hallway noises instead of fully paying attention. Which is frustrating, because someone could just be talking about, I don’t know, a software rollout or what they brought for lunch, and I’m sitting there acting normal but my brain is doing security checks.\n\nSo it’s not only what’s around me. The surroundings kind of give me things to latch onto, but the anxious part feels like it’s already running in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety is something that you feel is coming from inside you or is it usually triggered by things happening around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s coming from inside me, mostly, but then stuff around me gives it somewhere to go, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can be on the train, or in a meeting, or even just eating lunch with my team—and I’m still automatically noticing exits, who’s standing too close, listening for sounds in the hallway instead of really being there. Which is awkward, because people are just talking normally and I’m trying to look like I’m following along.\n\nSorry, I know I keep coming back to the same examples. It’s just the easiest way to explain it. It doesn’t always feel like something outside has to happen first. It’s more like I’m already on alert, and then the environment gives me things to scan.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety something that you feel all the time, or are there moments when you feel completely calm and relaxed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel completely calm that often. I mean, there are probably moments where I look calm, like I’m sitting at lunch with my team and nodding along, but part of me is still… checking things.\n\nLike on the train, in meetings, even at lunch, I automatically clock the exits, who’s standing too close, and I’m half-listening to noises in the hallway instead of really being present. It’s kind of like when my laptop has a background process running and everything seems fine until you notice it’s draining the battery—sorry, work analogy. But that’s what it feels like.\n\nSo, no, not completely relaxed most of the time. There are quieter moments, but I’m usually still a little on alert.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I mean, even in normal places, I’m automatically noticing the exits, watching if someone is standing too close, keeping one ear on hallway sounds or footsteps instead of really settling in.\n\nSo I think time off might make the work pressure less, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel completely relaxed. That alert feeling would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be the same if you were in a completely different environment, unrelated to work?\n\nPatient: I think it would be… not exactly the same, maybe, but pretty close. Like the place changes, but my brain still does the same routine.\n\nEven on the train, or when I’m in a meeting, or just at lunch with my team—sorry, that’s still work-adjacent, but even when it’s casual—I’m automatically checking where the exits are, noticing who’s standing too near me, listening for hallway sounds instead of just being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s kind of annoying because part of me knows nothing is happening, but I’m still doing the scan anyway. So a different environment might have different triggers, I guess, but I don’t think it would just go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you were to change your job or work environment to one that is very quiet and has minimal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? If it was quieter and there weren’t as many responsibilities, I’d probably have less work stuff to react to. Like fewer people needing things from me.\n\nBut I don’t think it would be significantly reduced, exactly, because I still do the same thing in different places. On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of really being present.\n\nAnd quiet can be weird too, actually—like sometimes when it’s too quiet, I notice every little sound more. Sorry, that’s kind of a tangent. I just mean… changing jobs might take off some pressure, but I don’t think it would shut off that alertness.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety is more related to your own thoughts and feelings or to the things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… both, but maybe it starts in me? Like, nothing has to be happening exactly, and I’ll still be doing it.\n\nOn the train, in meetings, even just having lunch with my team, I automatically clock where the exits are, and I notice who’s standing too close, and I’m half-listening for hallway noises instead of fully paying attention. The surroundings kind of give me things to latch onto, but the anxious part feels like it’s already running in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety is something that you feel is coming from inside you or is it usually triggered by things happening around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s coming from inside me, mostly, but then stuff around me gives it somewhere to go, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can be on the train, or in a meeting, or even just eating lunch with my team—and I’m still automatically noticing exits, who’s standing too close, listening for sounds in the hallway instead of really being there. Not really… I mean, maybe there are tiny moments where I’m distracted enough that I don’t notice it, like if I’m deep into some work problem, but even then it’s like part of me is still running checks.

Like I’m not relaxed-relaxed. I’m still kind of… ready for something. I mean, it depends what “enjoy” means, because I still go through the motions, like I’ll be at lunch with my team and everyone’s joking, or I’m on the train listening to a podcast I usually like, and part of me is trying to be normal and enjoy it.\n\nBut then I realize I’m not really in it. I’m automatically looking for exits, noticing who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on noises in the hallway or behind me. In meetings too, even when I’m not focused on the actual work part, I’m listening for hallway sounds or watching the door. Maybe some parts would be less, like not having meetings, or deadlines, or the office hallways—actually the hallway at work has this echo that makes every footstep sound closer than it is, which is probably not what you asked.\n\nBut the checking thing happens outside of work too. On the train, at lunch with my team, in meetings, I’m automatically looking for exits, noticing who’s standing too close, keeping one ear on noises around me instead of actually being there. So if I didn’t work at all, maybe there’d be fewer situations where it shows up, but I don’t think the anxiety would decrease significantly. It feels more like I carry it into places, not just that work causes it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly less if you were in a completely different work environment or role?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, depending on the environment. Like if it were quieter, or I didn’t have those long hallways—sorry, that sounds oddly specific, but hallways really do make me… aware.\n\nBut I don’t think a different role would make it significantly less. Because I do it everywhere. On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of actually being fully present.\n\nSo if the job were different, maybe the setting would bother me less in certain moments, but the scanning thing would probably still come with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious in a situation where you are completely in control and there are no external expectations or pressures?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Even if there wasn’t anything expected of me, I don’t know that I’d feel totally calm.\n\nLike, I could be sitting somewhere with nothing to do—no deadlines, no one needing anything—and I’d still notice where the exits are. Or who’s too close to me. I do that on the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team when people are just talking about normal stuff. And then I’m half-listening for footsteps or hallway noise instead of actually being there.\n\nSo… control helps a little maybe, but it doesn’t turn that part of my brain off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to take a break from work for an extended period of time, like a vacation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. A vacation sounds like it should help, and maybe it would in the sense that I wouldn’t have emails or meetings—well, unless I checked my email anyway, which I’m trying not to do, but that’s a whole other thing.\n\nBut even when I’m not working, I still do that scanning thing. Like on the train, or sitting with my team at lunch, or in meetings—actually meetings aren’t vacation, sorry. I mean, even in normal places, I’m automatically noticing the exits, watching if someone is standing too close, keeping one ear on hallway sounds or footsteps instead of really settling in.\n\nSo I think time off might make the work pressure less, but I don’t think I’d suddenly feel completely relaxed. That alert feeling would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be the same if you were in a completely different environment, unrelated to work?\n\nPatient: I think it would be… not exactly the same, maybe, but pretty close. Like the place changes, but my brain still does the same routine.\n\nEven on the train, or when I’m in a meeting, or just at lunch with my team—sorry, that’s still work-adjacent, but even when it’s casual—I’m automatically checking where the exits are, noticing who’s standing too near me, listening for hallway sounds instead of just being in the conversation.\n\nIt’s kind of annoying because part of me knows nothing is happening, but I’m still doing the scan anyway. So a different environment might have different triggers, I guess, but I don’t think it would just go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be significantly reduced if you were to change your job or work environment to one that is very quiet and has minimal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little? If it was quieter and there weren’t as many responsibilities, I’d probably have less work stuff to react to. Like fewer people needing things from me.\n\nBut I don’t think it would be significantly reduced, exactly, because I still do the same thing in different places. On the train, in meetings, even at lunch with my team, I’m automatically clocking the exits, watching who’s standing too close, keeping one ear tuned to hallway noises instead of really being present.\n\nAnd quiet can be weird too, actually—like sometimes when it’s too quiet, I notice every little sound more. Sorry, that’s kind of a tangent. I just mean… changing jobs might take off some pressure, but I don’t think it would shut off that alertness.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety is more related to your own thoughts and feelings or to the things that are happening around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s… both, but maybe it starts in me? Which is frustrating, because someone could just be talking about, I don’t know, a software rollout or what they brought for lunch, and I’m sitting there acting normal but my brain is doing security checks.\n\nSo it’s not only what’s around me. The surroundings kind of give me things to latch onto, but the anxious part feels like it’s already running in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety is something that you feel is coming from inside you or is it usually triggered by things happening around you?\n\nPatient: I think it’s coming from inside me, mostly, but then stuff around me gives it somewhere to go, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I can be on the train, or in a meeting, or even just eating lunch with my team—and I’m still automatically noticing exits, who’s standing too close, listening for sounds in the hallway instead of really being there. Which is awkward, because people are just talking normally and I’m trying to look like I’m following along.\n\nSorry, I know I keep coming back to the same examples. It’s just the easiest way to explain it. It doesn’t always feel like something outside has to happen first. It’s more like I’m already on alert, and then the environment gives me things to scan.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety something that you feel all the time, or are there moments when you feel completely calm and relaxed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I feel completely calm that often. I mean, there are probably moments where I look calm, like I’m sitting at lunch with my team and nodding along, but part of me is still… checking things.\n\nLike on the train, in meetings, even at lunch, I automatically clock the exits, who’s standing too close, and I’m half-listening to noises in the hallway instead of really being present. It’s kind of like when my laptop has a background process running and everything seems fine until you notice it’s draining the battery—sorry, work analogy.
